=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N towns, numbered 1,2,\ldots,N, arranged in a line in this order.
There are N-1 roads connecting adjacent towns: road j\,(1 \leq j \leq N-1) connects towns j and j+1. For each road j, you can set a strength w_j (an integer that may be negative).
When a person travels along a road, their stamina changes. Specifically, if a person with stamina x travels along road j, their stamina becomes x + w_j.
There are M people who will now move between these towns.
Person i\,(1 \le i \le M) starts with stamina 0 at town S_i and travels to town T_i via the shortest path.
It is guaranteed that |S_i - T_i| > 1. Also, (S_i, T_i) \neq (S_j, T_j) if i \neq j.
Person i’s requirement is as follows:

When departing Town S_i and when arriving at Town T_i, their stamina should be exactly 0. At every other town, their stamina should always be a positive integer.

Assume that there are no changes to stamina other than those due to traveling along roads as described above.
Process Q queries. For the k-th query (1 \le k \le Q), if it is possible to set the strengths of the roads so that the requirements of all people L_k, L_k + 1, \ldots, R_k are satisfied, print Yes; otherwise, print No.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M Q
S_1 T_1
S_2 T_2
\vdots
S_M T_M
L_1 R_1
L_2 R_2
\vdots
L_Q R_Q

Output

Print Q lines.
The k-th line should contain Yes if there is a way to set the strengths of the roads so that the requirements of all people L_k, L_k + 1, \ldots, R_k are satisfied, and No otherwise.

Constraints


- 3 \le N \le 4 \times 10^5
- 1 \le M \le 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \le Q \le 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \le S_i, T_i \le N
- |S_i - T_i| > 1
- (S_i, T_i) \neq (S_j, T_j)\,(i \neq j)
- 1 \le L_k \le R_k \le M
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5 4 2
4 2
1 3
3 5
2 4
1 3
2 4

Sample Output 1

Yes
No

For the first query, consider setting the strengths of roads 1, 2, 3, 4 to 1, -1, 1, -1, respectively.

- Person 1 starts at town 4 with stamina 0, visits town 3 with stamina 1, and arrives at town 2 with stamina 0.
- Person 2 starts at town 1 with stamina 0, visits town 2 with stamina 1, and arrives at town 3 with stamina 0.
- Person 3 starts at town 3 with stamina 0, visits town 4 with stamina 1, and arrives at town 5 with stamina 0.

Thus, this configuration satisfies the requirements of persons 1,2,3, so print Yes on the first line.
For the second query, it is impossible to satisfy the requirements of persons 2,3,4 simultaneously, so print No.

Sample Input 2

7 6 3
1 5
2 4
4 6
7 1
5 3
1 6
1 6
4 4
2 5

Sample Output 2

No
Yes
Yes

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

If two intervals overlap such that an endpoint of one lies inside another and the other's endpoint lies inside the first? More generally, if intervals cross/nest with opposite? Let's derive pairwise satisfiability.

Consider two persons i,j with intervals I=[a,b], J=[c,d], orientations.

Equality edges: P_a=P_b, P_c=P_d. If a,b,c,d not connected except maybe shared endpoints. Pairwise inequality constraints:
- For i: interior I^o values relative to P_a.
- For j: interior J^o values relative to P_c.

Let's derive pairwise compatibility first.

Given two people i,j with intervals I=[a,b], J=[c,d], orientations o_i,o_j ∈ {LR,RL}. Let endpoint values A=P_a=P_b, C=P_c=P_d. Constraints:
- If LR: for x in I^o, P_x > A.
- If RL: for x in I^o, P_x < A.
Similarly J.

We can ask if there exist integer values for positions satisfying both. Since only two equalities, components: if intervals share endpoints, A and C may be same component. Actually if a or b equals c or d, then A=C. If no shared endpoint, A,C distinct components. Interior positions may overlap; each position in overlap is constrained by both. Need no position with contradictory inequalities relative to A,C, and no equality conflict if an endpoint of one lies in interior of other and A=C? Let's formulate.

For any position x:
- If x in I^o: sign(P_x - A) = s_i (where s_i=+1 for LR, -1 for RL).
- If x in J^o: sign(P_x - C) = s_j.

If x in both interiors: need sign(P_x - A)=s_i and sign(P_x - C)=s_j. This is possible iff there exists value P_x satisfying two strict inequalities. Since P_x can be chosen independently for each component unless x connected by equality. For a single position, possible iff not (s_i=+ and A >=? Let's translate):
s_i=+ => P_x > A.
s_i=- => P_x < A.
s_j=+ => P_x > C.
s_j=- => P_x < C.
If s_i=+, s_j=+: need P_x > max(A,C), possible.
s_i=-, s_j=-: need P_x < min(A,C), possible.
s_i=+, s_j=-: need A < P_x < C. Possible iff A < C.
s_i=-, s_j=+: need C < P_x < A. Possible iff C < A.
So overlap interior positions impose inequalities between A and C depending on orientations:
- Same orientation (both LR or both RL): no constraint between A,C (always possible).
- Different orientations: if I LR (s=+), J RL (s=-): need A < C. If I RL, J LR: need C < A. (Because P_x between).

But if overlap contains multiple positions, same constraint. If no overlap, no constraint.

If an endpoint of J lies in I^o (and not equal to endpoint of I): Then x = c or d, P_x=C. I imposes sign(C - A)=s_i. This gives inequality between A,C:
- I LR: C > A.
- I RL: C < A.
If endpoint of I lies in J^o: sign(A - C)=s_j:
- J LR: A > C.
- J RL: A < C.

If both happen (crossing endpoints), constraints may conflict depending orientations.

Also if an endpoint of J lies in I^o and that endpoint is also connected to C; if c or d equals a/b? Then A=C and strict self conflict.

Pairwise satisfiability is existence of A,C satisfying all derived inequalities (and positions in overlap). This is a 2-variable strict inequality system, always satisfiable unless contradictory cycle A<C and C<A or A<A etc. Since only two variables, pairwise conflict iff there is a cycle of length 2 between A,C (or self-loop).

Let's list all possible constraints between A,C:
From overlap of interiors:
- same orientation: none.
- I LR, J RL: A < C.
- I RL, J LR: C < A.
From endpoint of J inside I:
- I LR: C > A (A<C)
- I RL: C < A (C<A)
From endpoint of I inside J:
- J LR: A > C (C<A)
- J RL: A < C (A<C)

If constraints include both A<C and C<A -> conflict. If only one direction, satisfiable. If self-loop (endpoint inside own? no) or A=C with strict? If endpoint of J inside I and also endpoint of I inside J? That gives two constraints maybe same direction? Example both LR crossing: I LR endpoint? Let's use previous I=[2,10] LR, J=[1,7] LR. Endpoint of J (7) inside I => A<C. Endpoint of I (2) inside J => J LR: A>C => C<A. Conflict. Good.

If I=[2,10] LR, J=[5,8] LR (nested same orientation): J endpoints inside I => A<C (twice). No endpoint of I inside J. Constraint A<C only, satisfiable. Indeed choose A=0,C=1, J interior >C, I interior >A; positions in J interior >C> A. Good.

If I=[2,10] LR, J=[1,3] LR: J endpoint 3 inside I => A<C; I endpoint 2 inside J => J LR: A>C => conflict? Let's check intervals I [2,10] LR, J [1,3] LR. I: P2=P10, interior 3..9 >P2. J: P1=P3, interior 2 >P1. Since P3 is interior of I => P3>P2. P2 is interior of J => P2>P1=P3. Contradiction. Yes.

If I=[2,10] LR, J=[1,3] RL? J RL: P1=P3, interior 2 <P1. Endpoint J (3) inside I => I LR: C> A. Endpoint I (2) inside J => J RL: A < C. Same direction A<C, satisfiable? Satisfiable. Good.

Maybe because constraints are "between components" and each interval creates all interior components on one side. This is similar to "signed interval overlap" and maybe satisfiability iff the "overlap graph" is bipartite? Let's explore.

Let's model each person as an interval with a sign s_i = +1 for LR (valley), -1 for RL (peak). For any two people i,j, if their intervals overlap in a certain way, they impose an ordering between their endpoint values. Specifically, define relation between intervals:
- If an endpoint of j lies inside i (strictly), then endpoint value of j is on the interior side of i: if i LR then C > A; if i RL then C < A. This can be written C - A has sign s_i? Let's set sign_i = +1 for LR (interior > endpoint), -1 for RL (interior < endpoint). Then if endpoint of j in interior of i: (C - A) * s_i > 0. Because if s_i=+ => C>A; if - => C<A. So sign(C-A)=s_i.
- If endpoint of i lies inside j: (A - C) * s_j > 0 => sign(C-A) = -s_j.
- If interiors overlap and orientations differ: if i LR, j RL => A<C => sign(C-A)=+ = s_i? s_i=+, yes. If i RL, j LR => C<A => sign(C-A)=- = s_i? s_i=-, yes. So overlap with different orientations imposes sign(C-A)=s_i (same as i's sign). If same orientation, no constraint from overlap. Wait if i LR, j RL: s_i=+, s_j=-, constraint A<C => sign(C-A)=+ = s_i = -s_j. If i RL, j LR: s_i=-, constraint C<A => sign(C-A)=- = s_i. So overlap diff imposes sign(C-A)=s_i (and also = -s_j). Since s_i=-s_j.

So pairwise constraints on sign(C-A) can be + or - or none. Conflict if both + and -.

- If interval X contains an endpoint of Y:

So the containing interval's sign determines whether it is lower or higher than the contained interval. If A_i=A_j, then any person whose interval has the other's endpoint in its interior causes strict self contradiction. More generally, if A_i=A_j and there is any constraint sign(A_j-A_i) = + or -, impossible. Pairwise conflict. This can happen if:
- Intervals share an endpoint and one interval's interior contains an endpoint of the other (the non-shared endpoint). If A_i != A_j (no shared endpoint), pairwise conflict occurs if constraints require both A_j>A_i and A_j<A_i. Let's derive using endpoint containment and overlap.

Let's define for two intervals with no shared endpoints. Assume a < c WLOG. Cases:
1. Disjoint: b < c. No constraints, compatible.
2. Nested: a < c < d < b (J inside I). Then endpoints of J inside I -> constraint sign(A_j-A_i)=s_i. No endpoint of I inside J. Interiors overlap (c,d) (if c<d) and if s_i != s_j, overlap diff gives sign(A_j-A_i)=s_i (same). So only sign=s_i. Compatible.
3. Crossing: a < c < b < d. Then:
   - endpoint c of J inside I -> sign(A_j-A_i)=s_i.
   - endpoint b of I inside J -> sign(A_i-A_j)=s_j => sign(A_j-A_i)=-s_j.
   - interiors overlap (c,b); if s_i != s_j, gives sign=s_i (which equals -s_j).
   Constraints are sign=s_i and sign=-s_j. Compatible iff s_i = -s_j (opposite signs). If same signs, conflict.
4. I contains J? Already nested.
If a=c (shared left) or b=d etc, handle separately.

Thus pairwise conflict can be characterized by:
- If intervals cross (a<c<b<d or c<a<d<b): conflict iff s_i == s_j.

- If intervals share an endpoint:
   * If their interiors are on the same side of the shared endpoint (i.e., one interval contains the other or same), conflict.

Let's define each person i:
- l_i, r_i (prefix indices, 0<=l<r<=N-1, r-l>=2)

Pairwise conflict conditions:
A. Crossing intervals with same sign.
B. Shared endpoint with one interval containing the other's other endpoint (same-side sharing). This includes intervals sharing l or r and one l/r inside other. More simply, if intervals share an endpoint and are not "adjacent" (i.e., one interval's interior contains the other's non-shared endpoint), conflict. Adjacent sharing (r_i == l_j or r_j == l_i) is compatible.

But what if intervals share an endpoint and have same interval? Same interval opposite sign conflict; same interval same sign duplicate not allowed. Let's formalize pairwise conflict algorithm for two intervals:
Let a=l_i,b=r_i,c=l_j,d=r_j.

If a==c and b==d:
   conflict if s_i != s_j. (If same sign duplicate impossible; but if same sign, no conflict)
If a==c: shared left. Since b != d. Suppose b<d (I shorter). Then b is inside J interior (c,d) -> conflict. If d<b, d inside I interior -> conflict. So any shared left with different right endpoint -> conflict.
If b==d: shared right. If a<c, c inside I interior -> conflict; if c<a, a inside J interior -> conflict. So conflict.
If a==d: I's right equals J's left (adjacent). Interiors (a,b) and (c,d) with c=b? Actually a==d means I=[a,b], J=[c,a]? Since d=a, c<d=a<b. J is left of I, sharing I's left? Wait a==d: I left endpoint a equals J right endpoint d. Intervals: J=[c,a], I=[a,b], adjacent at a. No endpoint inside other's interior (a boundary). Compatible.
If b==c: I right equals J left, adjacent. Compatible.
If no shared endpoints:
   If b < c or d < a: disjoint compatible.
   Else intervals overlap.
   Determine crossing vs nested:
   If a < c and d < b: J nested inside I -> compatible.
   If c < a and b < d: I nested inside J -> compatible.
   Else crossing (a<c<b<d or c<a<d<b): conflict if s_i == s_j.
This seems complete.

- If r_j == r_i and l_j != l_i: conflict.

So Type 2 query: max previous index with l_j == l_i OR r_j == r_i. We can maintain dictionaries mapping endpoint position -> max index seen so far with l or r equal to that position. Type 1: Crossing same sign. Need find previous j with same sign s and intervals crossing with current: either l_j < l_i < r_j < r_i (j starts before, ends inside current) or l_i < l_j < r_i < r_j (current starts before, ends inside j). More generally crossing means exactly one endpoint of each lies inside the other: (l_j < l_i < r_j < r_i) OR (l_i < l_j < r_i < r_j). Since l<r. Need query among previous people with same sign s_i, maximum index j such that intervals cross.

This is a 2D orthogonal range query over intervals. We process online by index; need max index. M 2e5, N 4e5. Need O(log^2 M) per query maybe.

Let's define for each person i, interval (l_i, r_i). Crossing with same sign:
Case A: l_j < l_i and l_i < r_j < r_i. (j's right endpoint inside current, j's left outside left)
Case B: l_i < l_j < r_i and r_j > r_i. (j's left endpoint inside current, j's right outside right)
These are symmetric. Note if l_j < l_i and r_j > r_i, then current nested inside j, not crossing. If l_j > l_i and r_j < r_i, j nested inside current, not crossing. If l_j < l_i and r_j < l_i disjoint? no crossing. If r_j == l_i? share boundary? Then not crossing (adjacent? Actually l_j < l_i and r_j = l_i, intervals adjacent, no conflict type1; type2? shared endpoint r_j = l_i adjacent compatible). So strict inequalities.

So for current i, crossing previous j same sign if:
A: l_j < l_i AND l_i < r_j < r_i.

Pseudo:
def cdq(arr, l, r):
    if r-l <= 1: return
    mid = (l+r)//2
    cdq(arr,l,mid)
    cdq(arr,mid,r)
    # compute contributions from left half positions [l,mid) to right half [mid,r)
    # condition A and B using segment tree
    # Need reset segment tree after each pass? We'll use a segment tree with timestamp or clear updated positions.
    # For condition A:
    left = arr[l:mid], right=arr[mid:r]
    sort left by l_i ascending, right by l_i ascending.
    seg = SegmentTreeMax over r coordinate (0..N-1) initially -1.
    ptr=0
    for query in right sorted by l:
        while ptr < len(left) and left[ptr].l < query.l:
            seg.update(left[ptr].r, left[ptr].pos)  # store max pos
            ptr += 1
        val = seg.query(query.l+1, query.r-1)
        if val > cross_bad[query.pos]: cross_bad[query.pos]=val
    # Need clear seg. We can reinitialize a new segment tree for each condition/level? That would be O(N logM) maybe too high. Use an array seg of size 2*size initialized to -1; after pass, reset updated positions to -1. But updates are point max; if we update same leaf multiple times, resetting all updated leaves to -1 is enough. Segment tree internal nodes need recompute? If we set leaves to -1 and then rebuild? We can keep a list of touched leaves and after pass, for each touched leaf set seg[size+pos]=-1 and recompute ancestors? Simpler: use a segment tree with "version" timestamps to avoid clearing. For each node, store value and version; when querying, if version != current_version treat as -1. Updates set node value=max(old if same version else -1, val) and version=current_version. This avoids clearing. Segment tree range query checks version. Great.
    # Condition B:
    sort left by r descending, right by r descending.
    seg2? We can reuse same segment tree with new version (increment version) to avoid clearing.
    ptr=0
    for query in right sorted by r descending:
        while ptr < len(left) and left[ptr].r > query.r:
            seg.update(left[ptr].l, left[ptr].pos)  # store by l
            ptr += 1
        val = seg.query(query.l+1, query.r-1)
        if val > cross_bad[query.pos]: cross_bad[query.pos]=val
    # Note: For condition B, we add intervals with r_j > r_i, query l_j in (l_i,r_i). Segment tree over l with point update at l_j, range query l. Good.

Need ensure strict inequalities:
- Condition A: l_j < l_i, r_j > l_i, r_j < r_i. Sorting left by l, add while l_j < l_i. Query r range [l_i+1, r_i-1]. Good.
- Condition B: l_j > l_i, l_j < r_i, r_j > r_i. Sorting left by r descending, add while r_j > r_i. Query l range [l_i+1, r_i-1]. Good.

size = power of two >= N (N prefix positions 0..N-1). tree = [-1]*(2*size), ver = [0]*(2*size), curver=0.
update(pos, val):
    idx = pos + size
    # update leaf
    if ver[idx] != curver: tree[idx]=val; ver[idx]=curver
    else: if val>tree[idx]: tree[idx]=val
    idx//=2
    while idx:
       left=2*idx; right=left+1
       # Need compute max of children considering versions. If child version != curver, treat as -1.
       m = -1

       if ver[right]==curver and tree[right]>m: m=tree[right]
       if ver[idx]!=curver or m>tree[idx]:

       idx//=2
But if ver[idx]==curver and m < tree[idx]? Could that happen? We only update a leaf with increasing? Values are positions (increasing over time within a pass? query(L,R):
    if L>R return -1
    L += size; R += size; res=-1
    while L<=R:
       if L&1: if ver[L]==curver and tree[L]>res: res=tree[L]; L+=1
       if not R&1: if ver[R]==curver and tree[R]>res: res=tree[R]; R-=1
       L//=2; R//=2
    return res

CDQ function solve(lo, hi, items_by_l, items_by_r):
    if hi-lo <=1: return
    mid=(lo+hi)//2
    # Partition items_by_l into left_l (pos<mid) and right_l (pos>=mid), preserving sorted by l.
    left_l=[]; right_l=[]
    for item in items_by_l:
        if item.pos < mid: left_l.append(item)
        else: right_l.append(item)
    # Similarly partition items_by_r into left_r, right_r.
    # Recurse on left half with left_l, left_r; right half with right_l, right_r.
    # Then cross contributions using left_l/right_l for A and left_r/right_r for B.
But if we partition before recursing, cross contributions need left and right lists sorted by l/r. Good. Recursion will further partition.

If we create left_l/right_l and pass to recursive calls, the parent's lists can be discarded after partitioning? In Python, if we assign, parent still references until function returns; but we can `del` after recursion? The recursive calls need their lists. Peak memory along recursion path O(K log K)? Recurse left: it partitions left_l into K/4 etc. Parent's left_l/right_l remain in local variables while recursing, so memory includes all lists at current path: K + K/2 + K/4 ... ~2K. Sibling lists also? At root, right_l exists while recursing left, so K/2. At next, right_l of left exists, etc. Total O(K) per level? Actually root by_l K, left_l K/2, right_l K/2; during left recursion, left_l K/2, its children K/4+K/4, plus root right_l K/2. Sum ~2K. So O(K log?) no, O(K) per level? At each depth, total list elements across all existing lists maybe K + K/2 + K/4... ~2K. Good. So memory O(K).

Pseudo:
def cdq(lo, hi, by_l, by_r):
    if hi-lo <= 1: return
    mid=(lo+hi)//2
    left_l=[]; right_l=[]
    for item in by_l:
        if item.pos < mid: left_l.append(item)
        else: right_l.append(item)
    left_r=[]; right_r=[]
    for item in by_r:
        if item.pos < mid: left_r.append(item)
        else: right_r.append(item)
    cdq(lo, mid, left_l, left_r)
    cdq(mid, hi, right_l, right_r)
    # cross contributions using left_l (sorted by l) and right_l (sorted by l) for A
    # using left_r (sorted by r) and right_r (sorted by r) for B
But note after recursing, left_l/right_l still available. Good.

Implementation of cdq with by_l/by_r lists:

def cdq(lo, hi, by_l, by_r):
    if hi - lo <= 1:
        return
    mid = (lo + hi) // 2
    left_l=[]; right_l=[]
    # by_l sorted by l asc
    for item in by_l:
        if item[2] < mid:
            left_l.append(item)
        else:
            right_l.append(item)
    left_r=[]; right_r=[]
    for item in by_r:
        if item[2] < mid:
            left_r.append(item)
        else:
            right_r.append(item)
    cdq(lo, mid, left_l, left_r)
    cdq(mid, hi, right_l, right_r)

    # Cross A
    nonlocal curver
    curver += 1
    cv = curver
    ptr = 0
    # left_l sorted by l, right_l sorted by l
    for q in right_l:
        ql = q[0]; qr = q[1]; qpos = q[2]
        while ptr < len(left_l) and left_l[ptr][0] < ql:
            item = left_l[ptr]
            seg_update(item[1], item[2])  # update r with rank
            ptr += 1
        if ql + 1 <= qr - 1:
            val = seg_query(ql+1, qr-1)
            if val > cross_bad[qpos]:
                cross_bad[qpos] = val
    # Cross B
    curver += 1
    cv = curver
    ptr = 0
    # left_r sorted by r desc, right_r sorted by r desc
    for q in right_r:
        ql = q[0]; qr = q[1]; qpos = q[2]
        while ptr < len(left_r) and left_r[ptr][1] > qr:
            item = left_r[ptr]
            seg_update(item[0], item[2])  # update l with rank
            ptr += 1
        if ql + 1 <= qr - 1:
            val = seg_query(ql+1, qr-1)
            if val > cross_bad[qpos]:
                cross_bad[qpos] = val

But wait: In Cross A, we update segment tree with left intervals as we iterate right_l sorted by l. However right_l is sorted by l, not by original rank. The query q may have rank < some left? No, all left ranks < mid <= all right ranks, so q rank > left ranks. Good. The order of processing q by l doesn't affect cross_bad. We add all left with l < q.l. Good.

In Cross B, left_r sorted by r descending, right_r sorted by r descending. We add left with r > q.r. Good.

Need ensure `seg_update` uses current version `cv`, but nested function uses global `curver` variable. If we pass cv explicitly to update/query to avoid nonlocal? We can define seg_update(pos, val, cv) and seg_query(L,R,cv). Then no nonlocal. But function call with cv each time. We'll implement as nested functions using `curver` from outer? In Python, assigning curver in cdq requires nonlocal. Simpler: define a class SegmentTree with method set_version(v), update(pos,val), query(L,R). Method call overhead. Or define functions at top level taking tree, ver, curver. But curver changes.

Maybe implement segment tree operations inline in cdq? That would be messy. We can define `seg_update(pos, val, cv)` and `seg_query(L,R,cv)` as nested functions in `cdq`? They would be redefined each call, overhead. Better define at module level:

tree = [-1]*(2*size)
ver = [0]*(2*size)

def seg_update(pos, val, cv):
    idx = pos + size
    if ver[idx] != cv:
        tree[idx] = val; ver[idx] = cv
    elif val > tree[idx]:
        tree[idx] = val
    idx >>= 1
    while idx:
        left = idx << 1
        m = -1
        if ver[left] == cv and tree[left] > m: m = tree[left]
        if ver[left|1] == cv and tree[left|1] > m: m = tree[left|1]
        if ver[idx] != cv or m > tree[idx]:
            tree[idx] = m; ver[idx] = cv
        idx >>= 1

def seg_query(L,R,cv):
    if L > R: return -1
    L += size; R += size; res=-1
    while L <= R:
        if L & 1:
            if ver[L] == cv and tree[L] > res: res = tree[L]
            L += 1
        if not (R & 1):
            if ver[R] == cv and tree[R] > res: res = tree[R]
            R -= 1
        L >>= 1; R >>= 1
    return res

Need `size` global.

In cdq, call `seg_update(item[1], item[2], cv)`.

Potential issue: In seg_update, if leaf already has version cv and val smaller, we don't update; ancestors remain correct. If leaf version different, set. For ancestors, if ver[idx] != cv or m > tree[idx], set. But if ver[idx]==cv and m < tree[idx]? Shouldn't happen as argued. Let's verify: Suppose ancestor tree[idx] was set from a child that later? We never decrease child values. If updating a leaf with smaller val, no change. If updating a leaf with larger val, m may increase. So m >= old tree[idx] if ver[idx]==cv. Good.

Now, performance of CDQ with tuple lists. Let's estimate memory/time.

For each sign, by_l and by_r lists of tuples. Tuples are Python objects; K up to 2e5. Partitioning creates many tuple references (not new tuples) in lists. Memory okay.

Recursion creates many lists; total memory maybe O(K log K)? Earlier estimated O(K) per depth but lists at different depths coexist; actually at root, by_l K, left_l K/2, right_l K/2, by_r K, left_r K/2, right_r K/2. During left recursion, root's by_l/by_r still exist (parameters), plus left_l/left_r and their partitions. Sum root by_l+by_r =2K, left_l+left_r=K, right_l+right_r=K, next level left partitions =K, etc. At depth d, total list capacity ~2K + K + K/2+... ~4K. Tuples are shared references. So memory O(K) ~2e5 tuples plus list refs ~ few MB. Good.

Time: Partitioning by_l and by_r at each node: total iterations per level = 2K (by_l + by_r) across all nodes? Actually at each node, by_l size = node size, by_r size = node size. Now, let's revisit pairwise sufficiency one more time. Is there a known theorem? The constraints are "each interval is either a valley or peak". Pairwise compatibility might indeed be sufficient because the system of inequalities is a "2-SAT" on interval orders? Let's see if global unsatisfiable can occur with all pairwise compatible. We tried to construct equality chain and found pairwise conflict. I think it's true due to "no bad pair" being equivalent to "signed intervals are laminar and endpoints unique", and such families are always satisfiable by assigning values based on containment depth. Let's try to prove constructively for confidence:

If no bad pair:
- For each sign, intervals are laminar (no crossing) and no same l/r.
- Across signs, intervals may cross but only opposite signs; nested any signs; adjacent okay.
Can we assign values? Maybe define for each prefix position x, consider the set of intervals containing x. For each such interval i:
   If sign + (LR), x's value must be > endpoint value A_i.
   If sign - (RL), x's value must be < A_i.
If two intervals containing x have opposite signs, pairwise compatibility ensures their endpoint values are ordered so there is room: if + interval i and - interval j, then A_i < A_j (as derived). If multiple, the maximum lower bound (from + intervals) must be less than minimum upper bound (from - intervals). Pairwise compatibility of all pairs of intervals containing x might ensure every + endpoint < every - endpoint? Let's check. Suppose + interval i and - interval j both contain x. If their intervals overlap at x. Pairwise compatibility:
- If they cross, opposite signs compatible and imposes A_i < A_j.
- If nested, compatible but does it impose A_i < A_j? Suppose + interval i contains - interval j (nested). Pairwise relation: endpoint of j inside i => sign(A_j-A_i)=s_i=+ => A_i < A_j. Good. If - interval j contains + interval i: endpoint of i inside j => sign(A_i-A_j)=s_j=- => A_j - A_i positive? sign(A_i-A_j)=- means A_i < A_j. So in all cases, if a + interval and a - interval overlap (contain a common point), pairwise compatibility implies A_i < A_j. Thus for any x, all + endpoints < all - endpoints. Then we can choose P_x between max + endpoints and min - endpoints, provided strict integer gap. Need ensure max+ < min- by at least 1. If values are integers and pairwise compatibility only strict, there might be max+ = min-? Pairwise compatibility for a + and - interval containing x gives A_i < A_j, strict, so max+ < min- if finite. Since integers, at least 1 gap? If max+ = 1, min-=2, gap 1, choose P_x? Need P_x >1 and <2 no integer. Wait strict inequalities require P_x > max+ and P_x < min-. If max+ = 1, min- = 2, no integer. But we can choose endpoint values with larger gaps globally if acyclic. Pairwise compatibility alone might allow max+ = min-? It requires A_i < A_j for every pair, so max+ < min-. Since integers, max+ <= min- -1. Need P_x integer > max+ and < min-. If min- = max+ +1, no integer. But we can shift endpoint values? Endpoint values are not fixed; pairwise compatibility only imposes inequalities, not exact. We can choose endpoint values to ensure gaps at least 2 where needed. Acyclic system can be assigned with topological order; if there is a direct constraint A_i < A_j, topological order gives at least 1 difference, but if P_x must be between, need chain length. However we can assign values with larger gaps (e.g., depth) to satisfy all strict inequalities and intermediate positions. Since no cycles, possible. So constructive assignment likely exists.

Thus pairwise sufficiency holds.

Now, let's consider if type2 same l/r conflict is always pairwise incompatible even if signs opposite and intervals adjacent? Same l/r means same side, not adjacent. If same l and same r same sign duplicate impossible; if same l and same r opposite conflict. If same l and different r, the shorter's right endpoint lies inside longer's interior; because they share left endpoint, endpoint values equal, so longer requires shorter endpoint value >/ < itself, conflict. Yes.

Now, let's handle input reading fast. Use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` parse ints. M+Q up to 4e5, ints ~1.2M, split okay memory ~ maybe 20MB. We'll implement fast parser.

Code structure:

import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000)

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it); M = next(it); Q = next(it)
    lastL = [-1]*N
    lastR = [-1]*N
    type2_bad = [-1]*M
    l_arr = [0]*M; r_arr=[0]*M; s_arr=[0]*M
    sign0=[]; sign1=[]
    orig0=[]; orig1=[]
    for i in range(M):
       S=next(it); T=next(it)
       if S < T:
          l=S-1; r=T-1; s=0
       else:
          l=T-1; r=S-1; s=1
       l_arr[i]=l; r_arr[i]=r; s_arr[i]=s
       b = lastL[l]
       if lastR[r] > b: b = lastR[r]
       type2_bad[i] = b
       lastL[l] = i
       lastR[r] = i
       if s==0:
          sign0.append((l,r,len(sign0)))
          orig0.append(i)
       else:
          sign1.append((l,r,len(sign1)))
          orig1.append(i)
    # Segment tree globals
    size=1
    while size < N: size <<=1
    tree=[-1]*(2*size)
    ver=[0]*(2*size)
    # define seg_update, seg_query
    ...
    type1_bad = [-1]*M
    for items, orig in [(sign0, orig0), (sign1, orig1)]:
       K=len(items)
       if K <= 1: continue
       cross_bad = [-1]*K
       by_l = sorted(items, key=lambda x: x[0])
       by_r = sorted(items, key=lambda x: x[1], reverse=True)
       cdq(0, K, by_l, by_r, cross_bad)
       for rank, val in enumerate(cross_bad):
          if val != -1:
             type1_bad[orig[rank]] = orig[val]
    last_bad = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
       b = type2_bad[i]
       if type1_bad[i] > b: b = type1_bad[i]
       last_bad[i] = b
    pref=[-1]*M
    mx=-1
    for i in range(M):
       if last_bad[i] > mx: mx=last_bad[i]
       pref[i]=mx
    out=[]
    for _ in range(Q):
       L=next(it); R=next(it)
       if pref[R-1] < L-1:
          out.append("Yes")
       else:
          out.append("No")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

Define seg_update:
def seg_update(pos, val, cv):
    idx = pos + size
    if ver[idx] != cv:
        tree[idx] = val; ver[idx] = cv
    elif val > tree[idx]:
        tree[idx] = val
    idx >>= 1
    while idx:
        left = idx << 1
        m = -1
        if ver[left] == cv and tree[left] > m:
            m = tree[left]
        if ver[left|1] == cv and tree[left|1] > m:
            m = tree[left|1]
        if ver[idx] != cv or m > tree[idx]:
            tree[idx] = m
            ver[idx] = cv
        idx >>= 1

Wait, if ver[idx] == cv and m <= tree[idx], we don't update ver. Good. But what if ver[idx] != cv and m == -1? This can happen if updating a leaf but its ancestors have no other current-version children. Then we set tree[idx]=-1, ver[idx]=cv. That's fine; query treats -1 as no value. But setting ver[idx]=cv with tree=-1 means the node is "active" with value -1. Query checks ver==cv and tree>res; -1 not > res if res=-1, so okay. Update later with a child value will set m and update. Good.

However, if ver[idx] != cv and m == -1, setting ver[idx]=cv and tree=-1 means future updates to a different child in same version: when computing m, left/right maybe one has ver cv with -1, other has ver cv with value; m picks value. Good.

seg_query:
def seg_query(L,R,cv):
    if L > R: return -1
    L += size; R += size; res=-1
    while L <= R:
       if L & 1:
          if ver[L] == cv and tree[L] > res: res=tree[L]
          L += 1
       if not (R & 1):
          if ver[R] == cv and tree[R] > res: res=tree[R]
          R -= 1
       L >>= 1; R >>= 1
    return res

Now, in cdq, after cross A, we don't need to clear. For cross B, increment cv. Good.

Potential issue: The segment tree version `cv` can become large but within int.

Now, recursion and list partitioning. We can optimize by using local variables for speed:
def cdq(lo, hi, by_l, by_r, cross_bad):
    if hi - lo <= 1:
        return
    mid = (lo + hi) >> 1
    left_l=[]; right_l=[]
    # local append
    for item in by_l:
        if item[2] < mid:
            left_l.append(item)
        else:
            right_l.append(item)
    left_r=[]; right_r=[]
    for item in by_r:
        if item[2] < mid:
            left_r.append(item)
        else:
            right_r.append(item)
    cdq(lo, mid, left_l, left_r, cross_bad)
    cdq(mid, hi, right_l, right_r, cross_bad)

    # Cross A
    cv_box[0] += 1
    cv = cv_box[0]
    ptr = 0
    llen = len(left_l)
    # local seg_update, seg_query
    for q in right_l:
        ql = q[0]; qr = q[1]; qpos = q[2]
        while ptr < llen and left_l[ptr][0] < ql:
            item = left_l[ptr]
            seg_update(item[1], item[2], cv)
            ptr += 1
        if ql + 1 <= qr - 1:
            val = seg_query(ql+1, qr-1, cv)
            if val > cross_bad[qpos]:
                cross_bad[qpos] = val

    # Cross B
    cv_box[0] += 1
    cv = cv_box[0]
    ptr = 0
    llen = len(left_r)
    for q in right_r:
        ql = q[0]; qr = q[1]; qpos = q[2]
        while ptr < llen and left_r[ptr][1] > qr:
            item = left_r[ptr]
            seg_update(item[0], item[2], cv)
            ptr += 1
        if ql + 1 <= qr - 1:
            val = seg_query(ql+1, qr-1, cv)
            if val > cross_bad[qpos]:
                cross_bad[qpos] = val

Need ensure left_l/right_l are not empty? If one side empty? mid between lo and hi, both nonempty if hi-lo>1. left_l/right_l partition by rank; since ranks in by_l are within [lo,hi), and mid between, both nonempty. Good.

Potential issue: by_l and by_r lists contain tuples (l,r,rank). Partition by rank < mid. Since by_l contains all ranks in [lo,hi), yes.

Now, one subtlety: In CDQ, cross contributions should be computed after recursive calls. The segment tree version is global; recursive calls use versions too. After recursive calls, cross A starts with new version, so no interference. Good.

Before final, let's maybe try to prove pairwise sufficiency one last time by attempting to find a global conflict with all pairwise compatible using a SAT solver in head. The system of constraints is a set of strict inequalities between components. A global conflict is a directed cycle. Suppose there is a directed cycle. Choose the interval in the cycle whose right endpoint is minimal, as earlier. Let I produce edge u->v. Let J produce next edge v->w. We showed I and J cross. If I and J have same sign, pairwise conflict. If opposite, no conflict. If opposite, maybe continue. Could have a cycle of even length with alternating signs, no consecutive same sign. But what about non-consecutive intervals? Maybe they conflict. Let's try to construct a 4-cycle with alternating signs and no pairwise conflict more carefully, respecting equality components (endpoint components shared between consecutive intervals must be connected). In our previous 4-cycle attempt, we used same component X3 for I2 and I3 but they didn't share endpoints; invalid. For a valid cycle, consecutive intervals that share a component must have equality connection. In a simple cycle of components, each component is endpoint of two people. For the component to be connected, those two people must share at least one prefix position (or be connected via other people, but in the cycle maybe not). If they share a position, then the two intervals either share an endpoint. This may create type2 conflict if same-side, or adjacent if opposite sides. Let's analyze.

In a directed cycle, component X_t is target of edge from X_{t-1} and source of edge to X_{t+1}. The two people producing these edges both have X_t as endpoint component (if edge types chosen accordingly) or one has X_t as interior? Actually X_t appears in two edges: incoming edge X_{t-1}->X_t and outgoing X_t->X_{t+1}. For incoming, X_t is either interior (if previous interval LR) or endpoint (if previous interval RL). For outgoing, X_t is either endpoint (if current interval LR) or interior (if current interval RL). So X_t may be endpoint in one or both intervals, or interior in both? Let's see:
- If previous edge is LR: previous interval endpoint X_{t-1}, interior X_t. So X_t is interior of previous.
- If previous edge is RL: previous interval endpoint X_t, interior X_{t-1}. So X_t is endpoint of previous.
- If current edge is LR: current interval endpoint X_t, interior X_{t+1}. X_t endpoint.
- If current edge is RL: current interval endpoint X_{t+1}, interior X_t. X_t interior.
Thus X_t can be interior in both (if prev LR, curr RL), endpoint in both (prev RL, curr LR), or one each. If X_t is interior in both, no equality edge incident to X_t from these two people; X_t is just a singleton component, but it appears as interior in two intervals. That's okay; no equality connection needed. If X_t is endpoint in at least one, the corresponding person's endpoints are X_t positions. If both intervals have X_t as endpoint, they need share a component; their endpoint sets must be connected. They could share a prefix position or be connected via other people. In a minimal cycle, perhaps they share a position, causing adjacency/type2.

Now, let's consider if the recursion cdq with lists might be slow due to Python recursion and list comprehensions. We can optimize partition using list comprehensions:

left_l = [item for item in by_l if item[2] < mid]
right_l = [item for item in by_l if item[2] >= mid]

left_l=[]; right_l=[]
for item in by_l:
    if item[2] < mid:
        left_l.append(item)
    else:
        right_l.append(item)
This is one pass. Append method lookup each time; can localize `l_append=left_l.append`, `r_append=right_l.append`:
left_l=[]; right_l=[]
la=left_l.append; ra=right_l.append
for item in by_l:
    if item[2] < mid: la(item)
    else: ra(item)
Similarly by_r. This is faster.

        if ver[idx] == cv and m <= tree[idx]:
            break
        tree[idx] = m
        ver[idx] = cv
        idx >>= 1

Now, let's write seg_update with local variables:
def seg_update(pos, val, cv, tree=tree, ver=ver, size=size):
    idx = pos + size
    if ver[idx] == cv:
        if val <= tree[idx]:
            return
        tree[idx] = val
    else:
        ver[idx] = cv
        tree[idx] = val
    idx >>= 1
    while idx:
        left = idx << 1
        m = -1
        if ver[left] == cv:
            tv = tree[left]
            if tv > m: m = tv
        if ver[left | 1] == cv:
            tv = tree[left | 1]
            if tv > m: m = tv
        if ver[idx] == cv and m <= tree[idx]:
            break
        tree[idx] = m
        ver[idx] = cv
        idx >>= 1

seg_query:
def seg_query(L, R, cv, tree=tree, ver=ver, size=size):
    if L > R:
        return -1
    L += size; R += size
    res = -1
    while L <= R:
        if L & 1:
            if ver[L] == cv:
                tv = tree[L]
                if tv > res: res = tv
            L += 1
        if not (R & 1):
            if ver[R] == cv:
                tv = tree[R]
                if tv > res: res = tv
            R -= 1
        L >>= 1; R >>= 1
    return res

import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000)

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it); M = next(it); Q = next(it)
    lastL = [-1]*N
    lastR = [-1]*N
    type2_bad = [-1]*M
    sign0 = []
    sign1 = []
    orig0 = []
    orig1 = []
    for i in range(M):
        S = next(it); T = next(it)
        if S < T:
            l = S - 1
            r = T - 1
            s = 0
        else:
            l = T - 1
            r = S - 1
            s = 1
        b = lastL[l]
        if lastR[r] > b:
            b = lastR[r]
        type2_bad[i] = b
        lastL[l] = i
        lastR[r] = i
        if s == 0:
            sign0.append((l, r, len(sign0)))
            orig0.append(i)
        else:
            sign1.append((l, r, len(sign1)))
            orig1.append(i)

    size = 1
    while size < N:
        size <<= 1
    tree = [-1] * (2 * size)
    ver = [0] * (2 * size)

    def seg_update(pos, val, cv, tree=tree, ver=ver, size=size):
        idx = pos + size
        if ver[idx] == cv:
            if val <= tree[idx]:
                return
            tree[idx] = val
        else:
            ver[idx] = cv
            tree[idx] = val
        idx >>= 1
        while idx:
            left = idx << 1
            m = -1
            if ver[left] == cv:
                tv = tree[left]
                if tv > m:
                    m = tv
            if ver[left | 1] == cv:
                tv = tree[left | 1]
                if tv > m:
                    m = tv
            if ver[idx] == cv and m <= tree[idx]:
                break
            tree[idx] = m
            ver[idx] = cv
            idx >>= 1

    def seg_query(L, R, cv, tree=tree, ver=ver, size=size):
        if L > R:
            return -1
        L += size
        R += size
        res = -1
        while L <= R:
            if L & 1:
                if ver[L] == cv:
                    tv = tree[L]
                    if tv > res:
                        res = tv
                L += 1
            if not (R & 1):
                if ver[R] == cv:
                    tv = tree[R]
                    if tv > res:
                        res = tv
                R -= 1
            L >>= 1
            R >>= 1
        return res

    cv_box = [0]

    def cdq(lo, hi, by_l, by_r, cross_bad):
        if hi - lo <= 1:
            return
        mid = (lo + hi) >> 1
        left_l = []
        right_l = []
        la = left_l.append
        ra = right_l.append
        for item in by_l:
            if item[2] < mid:
                la(item)
            else:
                ra(item)
        left_r = []
        right_r = []
        la = left_r.append
        ra = right_r.append
        for item in by_r:
            if item[2] < mid:
                la(item)
            else:
                ra(item)
        cdq(lo, mid, left_l, left_r, cross_bad)
        cdq(mid, hi, right_l, right_r, cross_bad)

        su = seg_update
        sq = seg_query

        cv_box[0] += 1
        cv = cv_box[0]
        ptr = 0
        llen = len(left_l)
        for q in right_l:
            ql = q[0]
            qr = q[1]
            qpos = q[2]
            while ptr < llen and left_l[ptr][0] < ql:
                item = left_l[ptr]
                su(item[1], item[2], cv)
                ptr += 1
            if ql + 1 <= qr - 1:
                val = sq(ql + 1, qr - 1, cv)
                if val > cross_bad[qpos]:
                    cross_bad[qpos] = val

        cv_box[0] += 1
        cv = cv_box[0]
        ptr = 0
        llen = len(left_r)
        for q in right_r:
            ql = q[0]
            qr = q[1]
            qpos = q[2]
            while ptr < llen and left_r[ptr][1] > qr:
                item = left_r[ptr]
                su(item[0], item[2], cv)
                ptr += 1
            if ql + 1 <= qr - 1:
                val = sq(ql + 1, qr - 1, cv)
                if val > cross_bad[qpos]:
                    cross_bad[qpos] = val

    type1_bad = [-1] * M

    for items, orig in ((sign0, orig0), (sign1, orig1)):
        K = len(items)
        if K <= 1:
            continue
        cross_bad = [-1] * K
        by_l = sorted(items, key=lambda x: x[0])
        by_r = sorted(items, key=lambda x: x[1], reverse=True)
        cdq(0, K, by_l, by_r, cross_bad)
        for rank, val in enumerate(cross_bad):
            if val != -1:
                type1_bad[orig[rank]] = orig[val]

    last_bad = [-1] * M
    for i in range(M):
        b = type2_bad[i]
        if type1_bad[i] > b:
            b = type1_bad[i]
        last_bad[i] = b

    pref = [-1] * M
    mx = -1
    for i in range(M):
        if last_bad[i] > mx:
            mx = last_bad[i]
        pref[i] = mx

    out = []
    for _ in range(Q):
        L = next(it)
        R = next(it)
        if pref[R - 1] < L - 1:
            out.append("Yes")
        else:
            out.append("No")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Potential issue: In cdq, `by_l` and `by_r` are partitioned; after recursing, left_l/right_l still sorted by l/r. Good.

Potential issue: In seg_update, if ver[idx] == cv and m <= tree[idx], break. But if ver[idx] == cv and tree[idx] is -1, m=-1, break. If later in same version we update a different child with value 5, we won't break because m=5 > -1. Good.

Potential issue: In seg_update, when ver[idx] != cv and m == -1, we set tree[idx] = -1, ver[idx] = cv. Then if later update another child in same version with value 5, m computed from children: one child ver cv tree -1, other child ver cv tree 5, m=5 > tree[idx] (-1), update. Good.

Potential issue: In seg_query, if a node has ver cv tree -1, and res=-1, tv=-1 not > res, so ignored. If res=0, -1 not >0. Good.

Now, let's think about if in CDQ, for condition A, we could instead sort by r and use a Fenwick over l with prefix max, and query l prefix? Condition A: l_j < l_i and r_j in (l_i,r_i). If we sort queries by r_i ascending, add left with r_j < r_i. Need filter r_j > l_i and l_j < l_i. We could maintain a Fenwick over l for all added, and another Fenwick over l for added with r_j <= l_i? But l_i varies; if we process queries sorted by l_i descending, we could remove? no.

Segment tree is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug in CDQ cross A due to using `right_l` sorted by l, but we need to process all queries; if right_l empty? In a node with both halves nonempty, right_l nonempty. Good.

Now, let's consider if by_l/by_r partition by rank < mid; since by_l contains ranks in [lo,hi), all items with rank < mid go left. Good.

Now, let's think about if recursion base hi-lo<=1; if hi-lo=0? Not possible because calls with nonempty. But if K=0 skip. Good.
